 \item {\csname fa0038Font0\endcsname\char"0057}{\csname fa0038Font1\endcsname\char"0068} two-layer neural network with step activations can represent the XOR function. However, when trained with gradient descent, it fails to learn XOR. What best explains this discrepancy?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item Step activations are insufficient to represent nonlinear decision boundaries
     \item The loss function is not convex for XOR
     \item The network requires more hidden units to separate XOR
     \item The activation provides no useful local gradient signal for weight updates
 \end{enumerate}
 
 \item {\csname fa0040Font0\endcsname\char"0057}{\csname fa0040Font1\endcsname\char"0068} {\csname fa0041Font0\endcsname\char"0069}{\csname fa0041Font1\endcsname\char"0063} language model is trained on the phrases ``small red car'' and ``large blue truck.'' During testing, it assigns meaningful probability mass to ``small blue car,'' which never appeared in training. What most directly enables this generalization?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item The model stores all observed word pairs explicitly
     \item Embeddings capture semantics that combine compositionally
     \item The softmax layer guarantees coverage of unseen sequences
     \item Training implicitly includes all possible word permutations
 \end{enumerate}

 \item What is the probability of the sequence $H \rightarrow C \rightarrow H$? Given:
 \[
 P(H \rightarrow H) = 0.7,\quad
 P(H \rightarrow C) = 0.3,\quad

 P(C \rightarrow C) = 0.6,
 \]

     \item $0.06$
     \item $0.21$
     \item $0.042$
 \end{enumerate}

 \item {\csname fa0219Font0\endcsname\char"0057}{\csname fa0219Font1\endcsname\char"0068} training, a neural network's loss decreases for a few iterations, then begins oscillating widely without converging. Which explanation is most consistent with this behavior?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item The batch size is too small, increasing gradient variability
     \item The batch size is too large, reducing gradient noise
     \item The learning rate is too small to escape local minima
     \item The learning rate is too large for the local loss curvature
 \end{enumerate}
 
 \item Consider a 4-word sequence $W = (w_1, w_2, w_3, w_4)$ with probabilities
 \[
 P(w_1) = 0.5,\quad
 P(w_2 \mid w_1) = 0.4,\quad
 P(w_3 \mid w_1, w_2) = 0.2,\quad
 P(w_4 \mid w_1, w_2, w_3) = 0.1.
 \]
 What is the perplexity of the sequence?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item $4.215$
     \item $2.154$
     \item $5.000$
     \item $3.976$
 \end{enumerate}
 
 \item A system has two states, $A$ and $B$, with the following transition probabilities:
 \begin{itemize}
     \item If in $A$: stays in $A$ with probability $0.6$ (and goes to $B$ with probability $0.4$)
     \item If in $B$: stays in $B$ with probability $0.4$ (and goes to $A$ with probability $0.6$)
 \end{itemize}
 Suppose the system starts in state $B$. As the number of transitions approaches infinity, what is the most accurate statement?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item The state probabilities converge to a fixed stationary distribution
     \item The state probabilities continue fluctuating without convergence
     \item The limiting distribution depends on the initial state
     \item The chain eventually remains permanently in one state
 \end{enumerate}
